<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-squaring** [claim](#stmt-lem-squaring) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-squaring): Elementary comparison of the purity with the excited trace tail. -> quadratic contraction of the time-doubling defect
- **step-criterion** [claim](#stmt-prop-criterion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-criterion): Rectangle identities, repeated squaring in both directions, then reflection-positive Hilbert space argument. -> uniform eigenvalue ratio bound and full gap lower bound
- **step-current-moments** [claim](#stmt-pre-current-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-current-moments): Compact score bounds, patch extraction of transverse current, Whitney summation over scales. -> logarithmic moment bound for the current
- **step-preliminary-mixing** [claim](#stmt-thm-preliminary) / [proof](#pre:part-12-3): Stiffness iteration by Gaussian augmentation, coarse orientations, disagreement exploration, association inequalities, bond interpolation. -> mixing bound and defect bound
- **step-all-temperature** [claim](#stmt-cor-all-temperature) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-current-moments-2): Spectral inclusion from covariance decay of local Lipschitz vectors and density in the reflected Hilbert space. -> positive full gap at every finite coupling
- **step-free-bounds** [claim](#stmt-free-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-free-bounds): Explicit alias formula for the iterated precision with shell estimates on a common complex domain. -> uniform ellipticity, analyticity and kernel decay
- **step-free-stack** [claim](#stmt-lem-free-stack) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-free-stack): Edge intertwiner with curl correction and two analytic divisions. -> positive completion with fine-output gain
- **step-core-reserve** [claim](#stmt-rg-core-reserve) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rg-core-reserve): Charge negative contributions to direct seed reserves in an algebraic energy ledger. -> positive energy reserve per seed
- **step-joint-factors** [claim](#stmt-rg-joint) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rg-joint): Joint Gaussian product estimate with transport integration by parts for hard factors. -> joint majorant bounds with derivatives
- **step-canonical-contraction** [claim](#stmt-rg-canonical-contraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rg-canonical-contraction): Wick graph order counting and triangular contraction in a fixed coefficient norm. -> bounded canonical orbit with convergent kicks
- **step-error-contraction** [claim](#stmt-rg-error-contraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rg-error-contraction): Gaussian guard, Taylor comparison with affine cancellation, direct bound for long labels. -> contraction of regular errors
- **step-rg-closure** [claim](#stmt-thm-rg-closure) / [proof](#stmt-thm-rg-closure): Connected-sum expansion, canonical map, normalization of constant and Hessian, ordered choice of constants. -> exact step in the retained class with coupling update
- **step-calibration** [claim](#stmt-thm-calibration) / [proof](#stmt-thm-calibration): Finite-torus Laplace expansion compared with the blocked expansion coefficient by coefficient. -> limits of the kinetic kicks
- **step-depth-calibration** [claim](#stmt-prop-calibration) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-calibration): Telescoping a potential function of the coupling along the trajectory. -> depth versus bare coupling relation
- **step-admission** [claim](#stmt-rg-admission) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rg-admission): Bound deviation from the drift potential, then a connectedness shooting argument. -> admitted trajectories ending in the terminal interval
- **step-matched-endpoint** [claim](#stmt-thm-matched-endpoint) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-matched-endpoint): Two-ended iteration of shape and coupling discrepancies with a common contraction rate. -> exponentially close endpoint densities
- **step-rarity** [claim](#stmt-cmp-rarity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cmp-rarity): Chessboard estimate from reflection positivity with stability and cap bounds. -> exponential rarity of flagged bond sets
- **step-exceptional** [claim](#stmt-cmp-exceptional) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cmp-exceptional): Flag-counting entropy bound combined with joint rarity. -> exponentially small exceptional set
- **step-forest** [claim](#stmt-cmp-forest) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cmp-forest): Inclusion-exclusion recursion encoded by rooted forests and tree-sum bounds. -> relative bound on covering sums
- **step-normalization** [claim](#stmt-cmp-normalization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cmp-normalization): Exact identity for the ratio of normalizing constants over a common set. -> partition function ratio bound
- **step-relative-comparison** [claim](#stmt-thm-relative-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cmp-normalization-2): Combine forest bound, exponent difference and normalization lemma. -> relative density and partition function comparison
- **step-cutoff-comparison** [claim](#stmt-prop-cutoff) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-cutoff): Apply the relative comparison at both box sizes; scalars cancel in the defect. -> defect difference bound between cutoffs
- **step-lower-bound** [claim](#stmt-thm-lower) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-lower): Pick one reference depth and box from growing trial boxes, transfer smallness to finer cutoffs, apply the criterion. -> lower gap bound in depth units
- **step-alignment** [claim](#stmt-lem-alignment) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-alignment): Rarity of rough bonds, Cauchy-Schwarz over three bonds, symmetry, pullback by conditional expectation. -> block correlation bounded below
- **step-upper-bound** [claim](#stmt-prop-upper) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-upper): Slab covariance bound from the full transfer gap applied to the pulled-back observables. -> upper gap bound
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-upper-2): Combine the two depth bounds with the depth calibration and the uniqueness result. -> sharp two-sided gap bounds

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-excited-tail-bound**: For the transfer spectrum on a cylinder of circumference w, the excited trace tail s_n(w) is bounded by e^{p(n,w)}-1, where p(n,w)=2 log Z(n,w)-log Z(2n,w) and Z(n,w) is the trace of the n-th power of the transfer operator. Equivalently, the purity P=Z(2n,w)/Z(n,w)^2 satisfies P(1+s_n)<=1.
- **inv-time-doubling-contraction**: If p(n,w)<=1/4 then (lambda_2/lambda_1)^n<=2p(n,w) and p(2n,w)<=4p(n,w)^2.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-contraction-outside-small-regime**: Applying the quadratic contraction of the purity defect under time doubling to a spectrum whose defect p(n,w) is larger than 1/4. -> The claimed inequalities are not guaranteed; the contraction can fail and a spurious spectral gap would be inferred.
- **anti-other-direction-without-doubling**: Transferring the statements for p to the spatial-doubling defect q without doubling the other period. -> The analogue only holds for q when the other period is doubled, so the conclusion is unjustified otherwise.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. Lemma 3 concerns a finite list of transfer eigenvalues and the explicit quantities p(n,w), s_n(w); given integer eigenvalues these are computed exactly and the stated inequalities can be decided by a finite procedure. Only this finite-spectrum lemma is tested; the paper's infinite-volume results are not.

```python
# INPUTS: stdin: first token n (integer >= 1), then integer transfer eigenvalues lam_1 > lam_2 >= ... >= 0
# PRECONDITIONS: lam_1 >= 1, lam_1 strictly larger than lam_2 when present, all values nonnegative integers, n >= 1
# OUTPUTS: lines Pn=, sn=, r=, P2n= (exact fractions) and a final RESULT line with tail_ok, sq_ok, regime
# TIME: O(k) integer powers of exponent up to 4n for k eigenvalues
# SPACE: O(k) big integers
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers and Fractions; 60-digit Decimal only for logarithm comparisons
# FAILURE: malformed input or violated preconditions raise an exception and give a nonzero exit status
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext


def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    n = int(data[0])
    lam = [int(x) for x in data[1:]]
    getcontext().prec = 60

    def Z(m):
        return sum(l ** m for l in lam)

    Pn = Fraction(Z(2 * n), Z(n) ** 2)
    P2n = Fraction(Z(4 * n), Z(2 * n) ** 2)
    s = Fraction(Z(n) - lam[0] ** n, lam[0] ** n)
    if len(lam) > 1:
        r = Fraction(lam[1] ** n, lam[0] ** n)
    else:
        r = Fraction(0)
    dec = lambda f: Decimal(f.numerator) / Decimal(f.denominator)
    p = -dec(Pn).ln()
    p2 = -dec(P2n).ln()
    r_dec = dec(r)
    tail_ok = Pn * (1 + s) <= 1
    small = p <= Decimal(1) / 4
    sq_ok = True
    if small:
        sq_ok = (r_dec <= 2 * p) and (p2 <= 4 * p * p)
    regime = 'small' if small else 'large'
    print(f'Pn={Pn}')
    print(f'sn={s}')
    print(f'r={r}')
    print(f'P2n={P2n}')
    print(f'RESULT tail_ok={int(tail_ok)} sq_ok={int(sq_ok)} regime={regime}')


main()
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves existence and two-sided order bounds for a lattice transfer gap and gives no executable procedure or interface. Its block map is an analytic proof device with existence-style constants, not a specification that software could implement. |
| games_simulation | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The full-gap scaling result supplies an analytic target for validating Monte Carlo simulations of the periodic O(4) model: the inverse gap should track the stated order of the natural inverse length. It is a benchmark for simulation output, not a simulation algorithm, so only adaptation is claimed. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper is a verification, analysis or measurement tool; the results are theorems whose proofs are not supplied with any checker or kit. A tool would need a finite decision procedure that the source does not give. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The block transformation and the shooting argument are existence and comparison arguments; the shooting step is stated with no uniqueness and no finite procedure. The conclusions are inequalities between limits, so no algorithm with complexity can be extracted. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces no storage layout or query structure. Its covering labels with supports, loads and inventories are bookkeeping inside a proof, with no operation costs or invariants a data structure could use. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No computation, dataflow or arithmetic circuit appears in the source. The results are about a lattice measure's spectral gap, with nothing a hardware design could be mapped from. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no adversary, hardness assumption or security notion. Exponential decay of correlations in a classical spin model gives no pseudorandomness or hardness property. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem, loss, data or generalization statement is present. A similarity of block averaging with multiscale models is only superficial and the source does not make it. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no objective to optimise and no feasibility problem. Choices of constants such as the reference box are made for a proof and are not an optimisation, and the paper says its thresholds give no useful numerical values. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No communication, failure model or consensus question is studied. Locality of supports and exponential decay of correlations are statements about a spin measure, not about message passing between nodes. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural home is mathematical physics, as bounds for cutoff theories only. The continuum spectrum needs separate convergence hypotheses on the transfer semigroup and block observables, so no application outside that setting is supported. |

          "exact_quote": "A label $\\lambda$ consists of a finite site support $P_\\lambda$, a load $s_\\lambda\\ge1$, and a fixed nonempty inventory $J_\\lambda$ of bonds whose endpoints belong to $P_\\lambda$. Its weight $k_\\lambda(q)$ is a bounded measurable function of $q|_{P_\\lambda}$; every eligibility indicator is included in this function. In particular $k_\\lambda(q)=0$ unless $J_\\lambda\\subset D(q)$. The support contains every spin read by the function or by an eligibility test, including a test asserting the absence of a flag.\n\n\nA family is compatible when its supports are pairwise disjoint. The mandatory covering sum is $$\\Xi(q)=\n \\sum_{\\substack{\\Gamma\\ {\\rm compatible}\\\\\n          \\bigsqcup_{\\lambda\\in\\Gamma}J_\\lambda=D(q)}}\n            \\prod_{\\lambda\\in\\Gamma}k_\\lambda(q).\n \\label{cmp:cover}$$ The empty family has weight one. Suppose a set of centered vectors $u$ dense in $\\Omega^\\perp$ has centered lattice approximants $u_j\\perp\\Omega_j$ and nonnegative integers $n_j(t)$ such that $$\\left\\lVert u_j\\right\\rVert^2\\to\\left\\lVert u\\right\\rVert^2,\\qquad\n \\langle u_j,T_j^{n_j(t)}u_j\\rangle\n       \\to\\langle u,e^{-t\\mathsf H}u\\rangle,\n \\qquad a_jn_j(t)\\to t$$ for every $t\\ge0$. <a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-periodic-state-only** (formal_scope): Uniqueness and the full transfer gap are established for the periodic local limit of the square tori only. Nothing is asserted about nonperiodic Gibbs states. Blocked use: ['Citing the result as uniqueness of all infinite-volume Gibbs states', 'Applying the gap to free, fixed or other non-periodic boundary conditions']
- **limit-cutoff-theories-only** (formal_scope): The mass bounds are statements about the cutoff lattice theories. Neither convergence of the masses nor existence of a continuum theory is assumed or proved. Blocked use: ['Reading the bounds as a continuum particle mass or a construction of a continuum quantum field theory', 'Using the physical-unit bounds without separate convergence hypotheses on the transfer semigroup and block observables']
- **limit-order-bounds-ineffective-constants** (approximation): The sharp scale is given only up to unspecified finite constants with 0<c<C; no exact asymptotic prefactor and no numerical value of the threshold or the constants is supplied. Blocked use: ['Predicting an exact correlation-length prefactor from the theorem', 'Using the constants as numerical thresholds in a simulation or certified computation']
- **limit-box-criterion-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The finite-volume gap bound requires the square-box defect on one even torus of side at least four to be below the fixed threshold. It is only a bound on a defect that must be shown small separately, and the final consequence for the full gap also needs uniqueness of the periodic state. Blocked use: ['Concluding a gap from a box where the defect has not been shown below the threshold', 'Using the dyadic-circumference conclusion for non-dyadic spatial sizes']
- **limit-ferromagnet-association** (hypothesis): The step from charged to arbitrary local observables uses association and correlation inequalities and holds only for the untwisted zero-field ferromagnet with nonnegative couplings. It is not claimed for pinned, signed or frustrated systems. Blocked use: ['Transferring the neutral-observable mixing bound to signed, twisted or pinned couplings', 'Treating the uniform bound under weakened bonds as valid for negative bond strengths']
- **limit-matched-terminal-coupling** (hypothesis): Two cutoffs are comparable only when their terminal kinetic couplings are equal. Large bare couplings alone do not give that matching condition. Blocked use: ['Comparing partition functions or gaps of two cutoffs with merely large, independently chosen bare couplings', 'Applying the cutoff comparison without the common terminal coupling in the fixed interval']
- **limit-thresholds-no-numerics** (unknown): The proof fixes finite thresholds by an ordered choice of inequalities but does not give useful numerical values for the reference depth, the coarse box or the largeness of the coupling. Blocked use: ['Quoting a numerical value of the coupling above which the sharp bounds hold', 'Using the reference depth or box size as an explicit algorithmic parameter']
- **limit-shooting-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The bare coupling reaching an exact terminal value is obtained by a connectedness argument; its existence is shown with no procedure and no uniqueness. Blocked use: ['Presenting the shooting step as a root-finding algorithm', 'Assuming a unique bare coupling per terminal value and depth']
- **limit-spin-dimension-and-dimension-two** (edge_case): The analysis is for four-component unit spins on a two-dimensional lattice. Quaternion commutator identities and the explicit two-loop drift are specific to this target and spacetime dimension. Blocked use: ['Applying the sharp scale to other numbers of spin components such as two or three without redoing the drift and current identities', 'Applying the result to lattices of dimension other than two']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove sharp mass bounds for the two-dimensional nearest-neighbor $`O(4)`$ model. For all sufficiently large inverse couplings <i>β</i>, the full transfer gap, including rotation-invariant local-observable sectors, is bounded above and below by positive multiples of $`\sqrt\beta e^{-\pi\beta}`$. We also prove that the model has a unique periodic local limit and a positive full gap at every finite <i>β</i> &gt; 0, resolving the all-temperature lattice mass-generation conjecture for this periodic state. Under the stated cutoff scaling, the mass bounds are uniform in independently fixed physical units. A continuum spectral interpretation requires separate convergence hypotheses for the transfer semigroup and the block observables.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:measure"></a>
Let $\Lambda_{n,w}=(\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z)\times(\mathbb Z/w\mathbb Z)$ be a periodic square-lattice rectangle, and assign a spin $\sigma_x\in S^3\subset\mathbb R^4$ to each site. We use normalized sphere measure $\,\mathrm d\omega$ and the probability law $$\label{eq:measure}
 \,\mathrm d\mu_{\beta;n,w}(\sigma)=Z_\beta(n,w)^{-1}
 \exp\left(\beta\sum_{\langle xy\rangle}\sigma_x\cdot\sigma_y\right)
 \prod_{x\in\Lambda_{n,w}}\,\mathrm d\omega(\sigma_x).$$ Every unoriented nearest-neighbor bond is counted once. Periods are even and at least four. Equivalently, the action, up to a constant, is $\frac\beta2\sum_{\langle xy\rangle}|\sigma_x-\sigma_y|^2$. The parameter $\beta>0$ is the bare inverse coupling. The number four refers to the internal spin space; spacetime is two-dimensional.

The principal question is how rapidly the model loses memory over long spatial separations. Three assertions must be distinguished: absence of magnetization, exponential decay of a particular spin correlation, and a positive gap for the full transfer operator. The third concerns every local observable, including the rotation-invariant bond energy $\sigma_x\cdot\sigma_y$. Its low-temperature scale is the subject of this paper.

The absence of ordinary ordering in two dimensions is a classical consequence of continuous symmetry. Mermin and Wagner established the foundational nonordering result for quantum Heisenberg systems, and Mermin gave the corresponding classical argument (Mermin and Wagner 1966; Mermin 1967) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[MW](#ref-MW), [Mermin1967](#ref-Mermin1967)]. McBryan and Spencer obtained quantitative algebraic upper bounds by complex spin rotations (McBryan and Spencer 1977) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[McBryanSpencer](#ref-McBryanSpencer)]; later extensions substantially weakened the regularity assumptions on the interaction (Gagnebin and Velenik 2014) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[GagnebinVelenik](#ref-GagnebinVelenik)]. These results leave open whether the decay is exponential. For two spin components the distinction is essential: the low-temperature phase predicted by Berezinskii, Kosterlitz and Thouless and established rigorously by Fröhlich and Spencer has no positive exponential spin-decay rate (Berezinskii 1971; Kosterlitz and Thouless 1973; Fröhlich and Spencer 1981) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Berezinskii](#ref-Berezinskii), [KosterlitzThouless](#ref-KosterlitzThouless), [FS](#ref-FS)].

At sufficiently high temperature, local Ward inequalities give exponential spin-correlation decay (Aizenman and Simon 1980, Theorem 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[AizenmanSimonWard](#ref-AizenmanSimonWard)]. For non-Abelian sigma models, perturbative renormalization further predicts asymptotic freedom and mass generation (Polyakov 1975; Brézin and Zinn-Justin 1976; Brézin, Zinn-Justin, and Le Guillou 1976) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Polyakov](#ref-Polyakov), [BrezinZinnJustin](#ref-BrezinZinnJustin), [BrezinZinnJustinLeGuillou](#ref-BrezinZinnJustinLeGuillou)]. In the normalization <a href="#eq:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:measure">[eq:measure]</a>, the predicted $O(4)$ inverse length is $\sqrt\beta e^{-\pi\beta}$. Bethe-Ansatz and $S$-matrix calculations of the exact continuum mass for $O(3)$ and $O(4)$, and subsequently general $O(n)$, provide a complementary description (Hasenfratz, Maggiore, and Niedermayer 1990; Hasenfratz and Niedermayer 1990) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[HasenfratzMaggioreNiedermayer](#ref-HasenfratzMaggioreNiedermayer), [HasenfratzNiedermayer](#ref-HasenfratzNiedermayer)]. They concern a continuum theory and its renormalization scale; they do not by themselves prove a gap for the specified lattice model or its convergence to that theory. The all-temperature lattice decay question is recorded as open in the 2025 account of Aru, Garban and Sepúlveda (Aru, Garban, and Sepúlveda 2025, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[AGS](#ref-AGS)]. We address the $O(4)$ model with its full local-observable transfer spectrum and keep the continuum issue separate.

OpenAI (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[OpenAIClassicalONDecay](#ref-OpenAIClassicalONDecay)] proves exponential spin-correlation decay for each fixed integer internal spin dimension at least three and each finite $\beta>0$. The bounds are uniform over finite free-boundary nearest-neighbor square-lattice subgraphs with edge strengths in $[0,\beta]$ and pass to subsequential local weak limits of free boxes with constant strength $\beta$. They neither identify these limits with the periodic state used here nor assert a full local-observable transfer gap.

The reflected Hilbert-space construction originates in the work of Osterwalder and Schrader (Osterwalder and Schrader 1973, 1975) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[OS73](#ref-OS73), [OS75](#ref-OS75)]; the elementary discrete construction needed here is given in Section <a href="#pre:part-12-2" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:part-12-2">3.12.2</a>. Reflection positivity associates to a periodic thermodynamic limit a Hilbert space, a vacuum vector $\Omega$, and a positive one-step translation contraction $T_\beta$, with $T_\beta\Omega=\Omega$. The Hilbert space is the completion of local observables on a time half-space under their reflected two-point form. Define the full mass gap by <a id="eq:mass-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:mass-definition}
 m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta)=-\log\left\lVert T_\beta\big|_{\Omega^\perp}\right\rVert.$$ This definition includes every sector generated by local observables, including invariant observables such as bond energies. A spin two-point estimate alone would not prove a lower bound for this gap. We abbreviate the natural inverse length by $\rho(\beta)=\sqrt\beta e^{-\pi\beta}$.

The theorem also gives bounds in terms of a coarse lattice. The block transformation used below introduces a unit spin for each $L\times L$ block through a normalized probability kernel, and then integrates the finer spins exactly. After $N$ steps, one coarse lattice spacing represents $L^N$ microscopic spacings. The negative logarithm of the resulting density is its effective action. The *kinetic coupling* is the bulk coefficient extracted from the quadratic cost of a slowly varying spin configuration in that action, in the same inverse-coupling convention as $\beta$. At the initial step it is $\beta$. The *terminal coupling* is its value when blocking stops.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:target"></a>
<a id="eq:depth-bounds"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *There are finite constants $\beta_0,c,C$, with $0<c<C$, such that for every $\beta\ge\beta_0$ the periodic square measures have a unique local limit, and its full transfer gap satisfies $$\label{eq:target}
 c\sqrt\beta\,e^{-\pi\beta}\le m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta)
            \le C\sqrt\beta\,e^{-\pi\beta}.$$ There are also a fixed dyadic blocking factor $L$, a fixed large terminal coupling $H$, and finite integers $N_0,M_0$ with the following property. For every $N\ge N_0$ one can choose a bare coupling $\beta_N$ whose exact $N$-step block transformation has terminal kinetic coupling $H$, and $$\label{eq:depth-bounds}
 \frac{\log2}{M_0L^N}\le m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta_N)
                  \le\frac{\log8}{L^N}.$$ The same bounds hold uniformly for terminal couplings in a fixed small interval about $H$, after increasing $M_0$ and $N_0$ if necessary.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-cor-all-temperature"></a>
<div id="cor:all-temperature" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Full gap at every positive temperature). *For every finite $\beta>0$, the measures <a href="#eq:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:measure">[eq:measure]</a> on periodic squares have a unique local limit. There are absolute constants $c,C>0$ such that its full transfer gap obeys $$m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta)\ge c\exp\{-\pi\beta-
 C\sqrt{(1+\beta)\log(2+\beta)}\}>0.$$*

</div>

Corollary <a href="#cor:all-temperature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:all-temperature">2</a> follows from the preliminary all-observable mixing estimate in Section <a href="#pre:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:section">[pre:section]</a>, without using the sharp-scale comparison. Uniqueness here refers to the periodic local limit; no assertion about every nonperiodic Gibbs state is included. For the $O(4)$ periodic state this gives a positive resolution of the all-temperature lattice mass-generation conjecture discussed above. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives two-sided order bounds, not an exact asymptotic prefactor.

<a id="eq:physical-main"></a>
In particular, fix a positive physical reference length $\ell_{\mathrm{ref}}$ and declare that $L^N$ microscopic steps have this length. The lattice spacing is $a_N=\ell_{\mathrm{ref}}L^{-N}$. Equation <a href="#eq:depth-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:depth-bounds">[eq:depth-bounds]</a> gives $$\label{eq:physical-main}
 \frac{\log2}{M_0\ell_{\mathrm{ref}}}
 \le \frac{m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta_N)}{a_N}
 \le \frac{\log8}{\ell_{\mathrm{ref}}}.$$ The units have been fixed by the block scale and terminal coupling, independently of the mass. Up to bounded positive multiplicative factors, the two-loop calculation identifies this choice with the natural asymptotically free scale for <a href="#eq:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:measure">[eq:measure]</a>. These are bounds for the cutoff theories; neither convergence of their masses nor existence of a continuum theory is assumed.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Methods and their relation to earlier work

The proof joins three traditions. Local rotation identities turn continuous symmetry into estimates on current fluctuations, as in the classical Ward-identity approach (Mermin 1967; Driessler, Landau, and Perez 1979; Aizenman and Simon 1980) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Mermin1967](#ref-Mermin1967), [DriesslerLandauPerez](#ref-DriesslerLandauPerez), [AizenmanSimonWard](#ref-AizenmanSimonWard)]. Here the quaternion commutator supplies a quantitative decrease of stiffness across scales. The later passage from charged observables to invariant ones uses Ginibre’s formulation of the Griffiths inequalities for Ising and plane-rotator systems, the FKG association mechanism, and embedded reflection variables (Ginibre 1970; Fortuin, Kasteleyn, and Ginibre 1971; Wolff 1989) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Ginibre1970](#ref-Ginibre1970), [FortuinKasteleynGinibre1971](#ref-FortuinKasteleynGinibre1971), [Wolff1989](#ref-Wolff1989)], followed by two successive conditioning arguments. We prove the finite forms needed below and retain the covariances of conditional means at both stages.

Reflection positivity connects Euclidean lattice correlations with a positive transfer operator. Classical lattice reflection and chessboard methods were developed systematically by Fröhlich, Israel, Lieb and Simon (Fröhlich et al. 1978, 1980) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[FILS78](#ref-FILS78), [FILS80](#ref-FILS80)]. The finite-volume argument uses the complete trace, so multiplicities and invariant sectors enter automatically. Its role differs from that of an infrared bound: it turns a small partition-function defect on one box into a spectral estimate uniform in larger circumferences. The positivity calculations and the limiting Hilbert-space argument are included in Section <a href="#sec:finite-volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-volume">2</a>.

The block-variable viewpoint goes back to Kadanoff and Wilson (Kadanoff 1966; Wilson 1971) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Kadanoff](#ref-Kadanoff), [Wilson](#ref-Wilson)]. Constructive results for sigma models have required careful distinctions of regime and model. Kupiainen established a large-$n$ expansion and a mass gap for sufficiently large $n$ above the spherical model’s critical temperature (Kupiainen 1980) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Kupiainen](#ref-Kupiainen)]; Gawędzki and Kupiainen constructed a finite-volume continuum limit for a hierarchical model (Gawędzki and Kupiainen 1986) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GawedzkiKupiainen](#ref-GawedzkiKupiainen)]; Mitter and Ramadas constructed the Wilson trajectory in perturbation theory (Mitter and Ramadas 1989) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[MitterRamadas](#ref-MitterRamadas)]. Bałaban developed small-field renormalization and variational-background methods for vector spins (Bałaban 1995, 1996) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[BalabanFlow](#ref-BalabanFlow), [BalabanVariational](#ref-BalabanVariational)]. For $SU(2)$, identified with $S^3$, Dybalski, Stottmeister and Tanimoto proved a small-field variational result for one block step, uniform in the box size (Dybalski, Stottmeister, and Tanimoto 2024b) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[DybalskiStottmeisterTanimotoVariational](#ref-DybalskiStottmeisterTanimotoVariational)]. These results are relevant predecessors of the block analysis; their hypotheses and conclusions do not supply the exact iterated $O(4)$ density estimates required here.

Normalized stochastic block transformations for sigma models also appear in the perfect-action construction of Hasenfratz and Niedermayer (Hasenfratz and Niedermayer 1994, sec. 2.1, Eqs. (3)–(5)) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[HasenfratzNiedermayerPerfect1994](#ref-HasenfratzNiedermayerPerfect1994)]. The observation kernel used here differs, and its iterated estimates are proved below. The renormalization step is an exact integration through that probability kernel. Its estimates concern the complete density, including rough configurations, rather than only a formal expansion near aligned spins. Perturbative coefficients determine the scale only after these uniform estimates are available. The comparison of two cutoffs then requires equal terminal kinetic couplings. Since the retained expansion has signed terms, we compare complete positive densities on a common set of large probability under both laws. These are the two interfaces that connect the coefficient calculation with the nonperturbative gap.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof strategy

There are two distinct changes of scale in the proof. At fixed $\beta$, we enlarge a periodic box to control the infinite-volume spectrum. To compare ultraviolet cutoffs, we instead change the bare coupling and the number of blocking steps while holding the coarse coupling fixed. The argument succeeds because the second operation preserves a finite-box estimate to which the first operation applies.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
##### A finite box controls the full spectrum

On a cylinder of spatial circumference $w$, let $K_w$ be the positive transfer operator that advances time by one lattice step. If its eigenvalues are $\lambda_1>\lambda_2\ge\cdots\ge0$, closing time after $n$ steps gives $Z_\beta(n,w)=\operatorname{Tr}K_w^n$. The ratio $$P_\beta(n,w)=\frac{Z_\beta(2n,w)}{Z_\beta(n,w)^2}
 =\sum_j\left(\frac{\lambda_j^n}{\sum_i\lambda_i^n}\right)^2$$ is the purity of the normalized spectral weights. When it is close to one, almost all the trace weight belongs to the largest eigenvalue. This controls the sum of the excited-state contributions, including their multiplicities. Writing $$s_n(w)=\sum_{j\ge2}(\lambda_j/\lambda_1)^n,
 \qquad s_{2n}(w)\le s_n(w)^2,$$ shows the elementary source of the improvement under time doubling.

<a id="intro:Delta"></a>
The remaining issue is that enlarging the spatial circumference could introduce new low-energy states. Transfer positivity in both coordinate directions resolves this issue through exact identities among rectangular partition functions. For a square define $$\Delta_\beta(n)=4\log Z_\beta(n,n)-\log Z_\beta(2n,2n).
 \label{intro:Delta}$$ By interchange of the coordinate axes, $$e^{-\Delta_\beta(n)}=P_\beta(n,n)^2P_\beta(n,2n).$$ The second purity is evaluated on the already-doubled rectangle. A product close to one therefore controls both directions. Section <a href="#sec:finite-volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-volume">2</a> proves $$\Delta_\beta(2n)\le64\Delta_\beta(n)^2,
 \qquad
 \Delta_\beta(n)\le\eta_*:=2^{-10}
 \ \Longrightarrow\ m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta)\ge\frac{\log2}{n}.$$ The recurrence applies in the displayed small-defect regime. Repeated squaring gives decay exponential in the increasing side length. It controls the full transfer spectrum, including every symmetry sector, as the circumference tends to infinity. This is the box-doubling part of the proof.

The same quantity is useful for comparing cutoffs. Any contribution $f_\beta nw$ to $\log Z_\beta(n,w)$ cancels from <a href="#intro:Delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:Delta">[intro:Delta]</a>. Thus the bulk free energy generated by exact integration does not enter the finite-volume test. Our lower-bound problem is now to make this one test small on a box whose physical size stays bounded as $\beta$ increases.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
##### The preliminary estimate: currents, stiffness, and mixing

The first source of decay is specific to the spin geometry. Identify $S^3$ with the unit quaternions. The first-order change of each bond energy under a spin rotation defines its current in that rotation direction. The corresponding *stiffness* is the second-order response of the free energy to the imposed twist. Its defining identity has the form “direct quadratic cost minus current covariance.” Conditioning on the boundaries of smaller squares gives the same structure: the stiffness of the larger square is an average of cell stiffnesses minus a covariance. A lower bound on these current fluctuations therefore improves an upper bound on stiffness. No positive lower bound on the local stiffness is needed.

Rotations about different quaternion axes do not commute. Their integration-by-parts identities consequently contain a term that rotates the current itself. Applying these identities over a range of wavelengths gives a stiffness reduction with leading term $(\log \ell)/\pi$ when the side of a square is increased by a factor $\ell$. The logarithm comes from a reciprocal-square frequency sum in two dimensions. Starting from stiffness at most $\beta$, one can therefore reach a small upper bound after a total logarithmic length close to $\pi\beta$. Choosing the intermediate scale increments to control the accumulated errors gives the length bound <a id="intro:Xi"></a>$$\Xi(\beta)=\exp\!\left[\pi\beta+
 C\sqrt{(1+\beta)\log(2+\beta)}\right].
 \label{intro:Xi}$$ The extra term is a loss in this estimate, not a prediction for the physical correlation length.

Small stiffness makes suitable local rotations inexpensive with high probability. Coarse orientation variables then admit a coupling that erases boundary disagreements except at rare locations. A disagreement can travel far only through a long connected chain of such failures. This gives decay first for observables with zero average under a common spin rotation.

To include invariant observables, write each spin as $(\cos\alpha_x\,z_x,\sin\alpha_x\,w_x)$, with $\alpha_x\in[0,\pi/2]$ and $z_x,w_x\in S^1$. Given the angles, the two circle-valued fields are independent ferromagnets. Further conditioning on component magnitudes reduces their sign variables to ferromagnetic Ising models. Positive-correlation inequalities relate their general covariances to two-point functions. At both conditioning stages the covariance of the conditional means must also be bounded; cutting distant bonds makes those means local, with a controlled error. This proves mixing for all local Lipschitz observables in the unpinned ferromagnetic systems, uniformly when bonds are weakened or removed.

<a id="intro:preliminary"></a>
That uniformity also compares the mean bond energy on the $n$ and $2n$ tori. Integrating the difference with respect to the coupling gives the precise output of Section <a href="#pre:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:section">[pre:section]</a>: $$0\le\Delta_\beta(n)
 \le C(1+\beta)^p n^p e^{-cn/\Xi(\beta)},
 \qquad n\ge C\Xi(\beta).
 \label{intro:preliminary}$$ This establishes a gap at each fixed coupling. Its length bound is too large to give a positive lower bound in the physical units of <a href="#eq:physical-main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-main">[eq:physical-main]</a>: the excess over $\rho(\beta)^{-1}=\beta^{-1/2}e^{\pi\beta}$ grows without bound. The next step uses this estimate at one reference cutoff and preserves its conclusion at all finer cutoffs.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
##### Comparing cutoffs at a fixed coarse coupling

Fix the blocking factor $L$ and a sufficiently large terminal inverse coupling $H$. Every step of the exact block transformation remains in the weak-coupling regime. The coarse law contains interactions beyond the original nearest-neighbor energy. These are retained, together with the contributions from configurations having large spin differences. The comparison concerns the complete coarse laws produced by the original lattice measures.

The basic contraction comes from averaging. In the linear prediction of the fine field from a slowly varying coarse field, each spin difference gains a factor $L^{-1}$, while a block contains $L^2$ sites. This contracts the dependence on inherited higher-order interactions, after their lower-order contributions have been extracted. Integrating the fluctuations also generates new interactions; the estimates keep them within the same controlled class. A quadratic term needs a further subtraction: its response to a uniform gradient is included in the running kinetic coupling. The remaining quadratic terms have a cancellation that makes them contract as well. A bulk constant is tracked separately. This is why the kinetic coupling is the parameter that must be matched.

<a id="intro:calibration"></a>
Let $I_H$ be a fixed small interval about $H$. A trajectory is *admitted* if every exact blocking step stays in the controlled class of densities and coupling ranges established in Section <a href="#rg:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rg:section">5</a>. Before constructing such trajectories, Section <a href="#cal:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cal:section">6</a> determines the two kinetic coefficients. The relation between depth and bare coupling is then determined, rather than imposed, by the kinetic drift. Computing the same finite-volume partition function directly and after exact blocking identifies the first two drift coefficients. Together with the remainder estimates for the exact transformation, this gives, with chronological step index $h$ and coupling $\widetilde b_h$, $$\widetilde b_{h+1}=\widetilde b_h-\gamma-\frac{\gamma}{2\pi\widetilde b_h}+r_h,
 \qquad \gamma=\frac{\log L}{\pi},$$ where, along an admitted nearest-neighbor trajectory, the total absolute error $\sum_h|r_h|$ is bounded uniformly in the depth. Summation, with $\widetilde b_0=\beta$ and $\widetilde b_N\in I_H$ fixed, yields $$N\log L=\pi\beta-\tfrac12\log\beta+O_{L,H}(1).
 \label{intro:calibration}$$ The inverse-coupling correction is essential: it supplies the factor $\sqrt\beta$ in the inverse length. Control of the leading exponential $e^{-\pi\beta}$ alone would leave an unbounded multiplicative uncertainty in physical units.

Section <a href="#sec:trajectories" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trajectories">7</a> next shows that for every terminal value $h\in I_H$ one can choose a bare coupling $\beta_N(h)$ so that all $N$ exact steps satisfy the required error bounds and the last coupling is $h$. Existence of these choices is part of the construction; uniqueness is unnecessary. Compare depths $N\ge K$. At the beginning of their last $K$ steps, the remaining interaction data can differ by a bounded amount. Their kinetic couplings agree at the opposite end. Forward contraction of the interaction differences, combined with this terminal condition on the coupling, bounds the differences by $C_He^{-c_HK}$ in the local norms controlling the retained interactions and errors. The estimate is uniform in $N-K$ and allows arbitrarily separated bare couplings.

Passing from local interaction bounds to partition functions requires an additional argument. The expansion uses signed terms, so a small absolute error in its coefficients need not be a small relative error in its sum. Section <a href="#cmp:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cmp:section">8</a> instead compares the complete positive densities on a common set whose complement has small probability under *both* laws. Local modifications of rough regions and an exact expansion provide this relative comparison. The probability bounds under both laws then control the normalization constants as well.

<a id="intro:comparison"></a>
The errors can accumulate over the $M^2$ coarse cells in a box. This explains both the strength and the volume restriction of the resulting comparison. Define <a id="intro:DN"></a>$$D_N(h;M)=\Delta_{\beta_N(h)}(ML^N).
 \label{intro:DN}$$ For all sufficiently large $K$, all $N\ge K$, and all dyadic coarse periods $M\ge M_{\min}(L,H)$, the estimate is $$|D_N(h;M)-D_K(h;M)|\le C_He^{-d_HK},
 \qquad (2M)^2\le e^{c_HK}.
 \label{intro:comparison}$$ The constants are uniform in $h\in I_H$. The factor $2M$ occurs because the test contains the doubled box as well. The common terminal coupling fixes the physical comparison: the two microscopic periods $ML^N$ and $ML^K$ both represent the same $M\times M$ coarse torus.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
##### Choose the reference box once, then refine the cutoff

We can now see why the two estimates fit together. Equation <a href="#intro:calibration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:calibration">[intro:calibration]</a> gives $$\frac{\Xi(\beta_K(h))}{L^K}
 =\exp\!\bigl[O_{L,H}(\sqrt{K\log K})\bigr].$$ Thus the coarse side required by the preliminary decay bound grows subexponentially in $K$. The comparison <a href="#intro:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:comparison">[intro:comparison]</a>, however, permits coarse sides growing exponentially in $K$. There is room to choose a box large enough for the first estimate and small enough for the second.

Concretely, choose dyadic $M_K$ with $$\log M_K=K^{3/4}+O(1).$$ Then $$\log\frac{M_KL^K}{\Xi(\beta_K(h))}
 =K^{3/4}-O_{L,H}(\sqrt{K\log K}),
 \qquad 2\log(2M_K)=o(K).$$ The first relation makes the decay in <a href="#intro:preliminary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:preliminary">[intro:preliminary]</a> overwhelm its polynomial prefactor, so $D_K(h;M_K)\to0$ uniformly in $h$. The second keeps both boxes inside the comparison range. The exponent $3/4$ merely lies between $1/2$ and $1$; no significance is attached to its particular value.

Choose one finite $K_0$ large enough that $$\sup_{h\in I_H}D_{K_0}(h;M_{K_0})+C_He^{-d_HK_0}\le\eta_*.$$ Now fix $M_0=M_{K_0}$. For every finer cutoff $N\ge K_0$, $$D_N(h;M_0)\le\eta_*.$$ Box doubling therefore gives $m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta_N(h))\ge(\log2)/(M_0L^N)$. The growing family $M_K$ was used only to find a single reference box. After that choice, $M_0$ is constant, and the microscopic box $M_0L^N$ always has physical side $M_0\ell_{\mathrm{ref}}$. This order of choices removes the subexponential loss from the physical mass bound. It also explains why the blocking construction can stop at a large $H$: long-distance decay is supplied by the preliminary argument on the reference lattice.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
##### A block correlation bounds the mass above

The upper bound uses a different consequence of stopping at a large $H$. Nearby terminal spins remain aligned on average, by the one-law rarity estimate of Lemma <a href="#cmp:rarity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cmp:rarity">20</a>. For terminal sites $0$ and $3e_1$, Lemma <a href="#lem:alignment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:alignment">29</a> gives $\mathbb E[V_0^{(1)}V_{3e_1}^{(1)}]\ge1/8$, where the superscript denotes one spin component. Pull this observable back to the original lattice by setting $$F_X(\sigma)=\mathbb E[V_X^{(1)}\mid\sigma].$$ Each $F_X$ is centered, bounded by one, and supported in its block of side $L^N$. The auxiliary variables in disjoint blocks are independent given $\sigma$, so the two $F$’s retain the same correlation. Their supports are separated by at least $L^N$ time steps. The full transfer gap bounds that correlation by $e^{-m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta_N(h))L^N}$, giving the upper bound in <a href="#eq:depth-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:depth-bounds">[eq:depth-bounds]</a>.

The proof thus gives a uniform exponential decay rate for all connected correlations of bounded local observables in physical units, together with a nonzero bounded correlation at a fixed physical separation. These give the two sides of <a href="#eq:physical-main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-main">[eq:physical-main]</a>. The admission argument assigns every sufficiently large bare coupling a trajectory ending in $I_H$, so the depth bounds imply the assertion for every sufficiently large $\beta$. Section <a href="#sec:physical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:physical">11</a> states separately the additional convergence assumptions needed to interpret the bounds as statements about an existing continuum theory.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### Conventions.

The normalizations used throughout are collected here to distinguish the physical spacing from the observation precision.

<div class="center">

| Quantity              | Convention                                          |
|:----------------------|:----------------------------------------------------|
| Spin and measure      | $\sigma_x=q_x\in S^3$; $\int_{S^3}d\omega=1$        |
| Bond sum              | Each unoriented nearest-neighbor bond counted once  |
| Bare parameter        | $\beta>0$ is inverse coupling (inverse temperature) |
| Running parameter     | $b$ is the extracted kinetic inverse coupling       |
| Preliminary distance  | Maximum-norm lattice distance between supports      |
| Transfer distance     | Number of time edges between the actual supports    |
| Physical spacing      | $a_N=\ell_{\mathrm{ref}}L^{-N}$                     |
| Observation precision | $a=1$, a dimensionless kernel parameter             |

</div>

For quaternion generators, an imaginary unit $u$ acts by $q_x\mapsto e^{tu}q_x$ and $[u,v]=2u\times v$; the bond current normalization is derived in Section <a href="#pre:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:local">3.1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### Organization.

Section <a href="#sec:finite-volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-volume">2</a> proves the box-doubling criterion and Section <a href="#pre:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:section">[pre:section]</a> proves the preliminary estimate. Sections <a href="#free:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:section">4</a> and <a href="#rg:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rg:section">5</a> construct and control the exact block transformation. Section <a href="#cal:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cal:section">6</a> calibrates its kinetic drift. Section <a href="#sec:trajectories" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trajectories">7</a> constructs admitted trajectories and matches their terminal couplings; Section <a href="#cmp:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cmp:section">8</a> compares the resulting positive densities. Sections <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">9</a>–<a href="#sec:physical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:physical">11</a> assemble the lower bound, prove the upper bound, and identify the physical units. The regularity and support conventions required by the expansions are specified at their points of use. The appendices are parts of the proof: Appendix <a href="#app:analytic-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:analytic-block">12</a> supplies the uniform analytic estimates for the linear block map; Appendix <a href="#supp:canonical-fixed-norm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="supp:canonical-fixed-norm">13</a> fixes the canonical coefficient norm and proves its diagonal contraction; Appendix <a href="#app:rg-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:rg-geometry">14</a> constructs the local factors and their complete supports; and Appendix <a href="#supp:joint-majorants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="supp:joint-majorants">15</a> proves the joint Gaussian product bound and verifies it for all eight prepared-factor classes.

<a id="sec:finite-volume"></a>
### A finite-volume criterion for the full gap

We first isolate a finite-volume statement that will be sufficient for a mass gap. It involves the actual partition functions, so it sees every spectral sector. It is also insensitive to a contribution to the free energy proportional to volume, which will make it suitable for renormalization.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Transfer eigenvalues and a measure of excited-state weight

Fix a spatial circumference $w$. A time slice is a configuration $s=(s_1,\ldots,s_w)\in(S^3)^w$. Set $V_w(s)=\sum_{i=1}^w s_i\cdot s_{i+1}$, with periodic indices, and let $K_w$ be the integral operator with kernel <a id="eq:transfer-kernel"></a>$$\label{eq:transfer-kernel}
 K_w(s,s')=
 \exp\!\left[\frac\beta2 V_w(s)+\beta\sum_{i=1}^w s_i\cdot s'_i
                         +\frac\beta2V_w(s')\right].$$ The operator is compact and self-adjoint. It is positive semidefinite: the expansion of $e^{\beta s\cdot s'}$ is a sum of positive tensor-product kernels, and multiplication on both sides by $e^{\beta V_w/2}$ preserves positivity. The positive-kernel series has finite diagonal integral, so it also proves trace class. The strictly positive kernel gives a simple largest eigenvalue. Write $\lambda_1(w)>\lambda_2(w)\ge\lambda_3(w)\ge\cdots\ge0$, allowing $\lambda_2=0$. Closing the time direction gives <a id="eq:trace"></a>$$\label{eq:trace}
 Z_\beta(n,w)=\operatorname{Tr}K_w^n=\sum_i\lambda_i(w)^n.$$

<a id="eq:p"></a>
<a id="eq:q"></a>
Suppress $\beta$ temporarily and define $$\begin{aligned}
 p(n,w)&=2\log Z(n,w)-\log Z(2n,w),\label{eq:p}\\
 q(n,w)&=2\log Z(n,w)-\log Z(n,2w).\label{eq:q}
\end{aligned}$$ Both are nonnegative. To interpret $p$, introduce the normalized trace weights <a id="eq:purity"></a>$$t_i=\frac{\lambda_i(w)^n}{\sum_j\lambda_j(w)^n}.
 \qquad\text{Then}\qquad
 e^{-p(n,w)}=\sum_i t_i^2.
\label{eq:purity}$$ Thus small $p$ means that almost all the trace weight lies in one eigenstate, necessarily the ground state. The same interpretation applies to $q$ after interchanging the coordinate directions.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-lem-squaring"></a>
<div id="lem:squaring" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *The excited trace tail $$s_n(w)=\sum_{i\ge2}\left(\frac{\lambda_i(w)}{\lambda_1(w)}\right)^n$$ satisfies $s_n(w)\le e^{p(n,w)}-1$. If $p(n,w)\le1/4$, then <a id="eq:squaring"></a>$$\label{eq:squaring}
 \left(\frac{\lambda_2(w)}{\lambda_1(w)}\right)^n\le2p(n,w),
 \qquad p(2n,w)\le4p(n,w)^2.$$ The analogous statements hold for $q$ when the other period is doubled.*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-squaring"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-squaring</summary>

*Proof.* Since $\sum_i t_i^2\le t_1=(1+s_n)^{-1}$, we have $s_n\le e^p-1$. For $p\le1/4$, this is at most $pe^p\le2p$, which also bounds the first excited term. The largest eigenvalue cancels from the definition of $p$, so $$p(2n,w)=2\log(1+s_{2n})-\log(1+s_{4n})
 \le2s_{2n}\le2s_n^2\le2p^2e^{2p}\le4p^2.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Increasing the spatial volume

Ground-state dominance at one fixed circumference is not enough. New low-energy states could appear as the circumference increases. The following exact identity lets us control that limit: <a id="eq:rectangle"></a>$$\label{eq:rectangle}
 p(n,2w)=2p(n,w)-2q(n,w)+q(2n,w).$$ It follows by substituting <a href="#eq:p" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:p">[eq:p]</a>–<a href="#eq:q" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:q">[eq:q]</a>. There is a second identity obtained by interchanging the directions.

Figure <a href="#fig:rectangle-doubling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:rectangle-doubling">1</a> records the two-direction geometry.

<figure id="fig:rectangle-doubling">

<figcaption>The two coordinate directions enter the square-box test. Every rectangle has periodic boundary conditions; the dashed lines mark its dimensions, not cuts that make the pieces independent. The second doubling starts from the enlarged rectangle. The displayed identity is exact, with the fixed coupling suppressed.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="eq:Delta"></a>
Define the square-box test $$\label{eq:Delta}
 \Delta_\beta(n)=4\log Z_\beta(n,n)-\log Z_\beta(2n,2n).$$ It obeys $\Delta_\beta(n)=2p(n,n)+q(2n,n)\ge0$. The coefficients in <a href="#eq:Delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Delta">[eq:Delta]</a> cancel any term proportional to the area of the box.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-prop-criterion"></a>
<div id="prop:criterion" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:width-gap"></a>
<a id="eq:criterion-gap"></a>
**Proposition 4** (Finite-volume criterion). *Let $n\ge4$ be even. If <a id="eq:small-test"></a>$$\Delta_\beta(n)\le\eta_*:=2^{-10},
\label{eq:small-test}$$ then the transfer operators on circumferences $2^kn$, $k\ge0$, satisfy $$\frac{\lambda_2(2^kn)}{\lambda_1(2^kn)}
 \le e^{-(\log2)/n}.
\label{eq:width-gap}$$ Every local limit of the square tori with sides $2^kn$ has full gap at least $(\log2)/n$. In particular, if the periodic infinite-plane state is unique, then $$\label{eq:criterion-gap}
 m_{\mathrm{lat}}(\beta)\ge\frac{\log2}{n}.$$*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-criterion"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-criterion</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\Delta=\Delta_\beta(r)$. Besides $q(2r,r)\le\Delta$, the definitions give $p(r,2r)=\Delta-2q(r,r)\le\Delta$. Lemma <a href="#lem:squaring" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:squaring">3</a> therefore yields $p(2r,2r)\le4\Delta^2$. The rectangle identity in the other direction gives $$q(4r,r)=2q(2r,r)-2p(2r,r)+p(2r,2r)
 \le2\Delta+4\Delta^2\le3\Delta.$$ Squaring in that direction gives $q(4r,2r)\le36\Delta^2$. Consequently, <a id="eq:quadratic-recursion"></a>$$\Delta_\beta(2r)
 =2p(2r,2r)+q(4r,2r)
 \le44\Delta_\beta(r)^2\le64\Delta_\beta(r)^2.
\label{eq:quadratic-recursion}$$ Every use of the lemma is justified by $3\eta_*<1/4$, and the smallness condition is preserved. For $n_k=2^kn$, induction gives $$\Delta_\beta(n_k)\le\frac1{64}
          (64\eta_*)^{2^k}\le\frac1{64}2^{-2^k}.$$ Since the first assertion of the lemma bounds $(\lambda_2(n_k)/\lambda_1(n_k))^{n_k}$ by $2p(n_k,n_k)\le\Delta_\beta(n_k)$, taking the $n_k$-th root proves <a href="#eq:width-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:width-gap">[eq:width-gap]</a>.

<a id="eq:slab-bound"></a>
Let $\Omega_w$ be the normalized positive ground eigenvector of $K_w$. For a bounded continuous observable $F$ supported in a time slab of finite thickness, let $K_{w,F}$ denote the transfer kernel across that slab with the insertion of $F$. Pointwise, $|K_{w,F}|\le\left\lVert F\right\rVert_\infty K_w^r$, where $r$ is the number of transfer steps across the slab. Set $A_F=K_{w,F}/\lambda_1(w)^r$; for $r=0$, this is multiplication by $F$. Its operator norm is at most $\left\lVert F\right\rVert_\infty$. The infinite-time cylinder expectation is $\omega_w(F)=\langle\Omega_w,A_F\Omega_w\rangle$. Applying <a href="#eq:width-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:width-gap">[eq:width-gap]</a> between two such insertions gives $$|\operatorname{Cov}_{\omega_w}(F,G)|\le\left\lVert F\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert G\right\rVert_\infty
                           e^{-(\log2)d/n},
\label{eq:slab-bound}$$ where $d$ is the number of transfer edges separating their supports. This bound passes to any local limit of the cylinder states as $w=2^kn\to\infty$.

We can identify these limits directly with the square-torus limits. Let $\widehat K_w=K_w/\lambda_1(w)$. If $w=n_k$ and $r\le w/2$, the square-torus expectation is $$\frac{\operatorname{Tr}(A_F\widehat K_w^{\,w-r})}{\operatorname{Tr}\widehat K_w^{\,w}}.$$ Separating the ground projection in numerator and denominator shows that its difference from the cylinder expectation is at most $2\left\lVert F\right\rVert_\infty s_{w/2}(w)$. But $$p(w/2,w)\le\Delta_\beta(w/2),\qquad
 s_{w/2}(w)\le e^{\Delta_\beta(w/2)}-1\longrightarrow0.$$ Thus the square and cylinder expectations have the same subsequential limits, also for products of fixed local observables. In particular, <a href="#eq:slab-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slab-bound">[eq:slab-bound]</a> holds in every such square-torus limit.

For each centered vector created by a local observable in the reflection-positive Hilbert space, the transfer autocorrelation has a positive spectral measure. It is a correlation of two reflected slabs, so <a href="#eq:slab-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slab-bound">[eq:slab-bound]</a> applies and excludes spectral support above $e^{-(\log2)/n}$ for that vector. Such vectors are dense in the vacuum orthogonal subspace, so the same exclusion holds for the full transfer operator. This proves <a href="#eq:criterion-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:criterion-gap">[eq:criterion-gap]</a>. ◻

</details>

The slab estimate extends to bounded measurable local observables by approximation in their finite-dimensional distributions. No prefactor depending on the size of a slab is needed; this will be useful for the upper mass bound.

The criterion has reduced the problem to a single question: can one make $\Delta_\beta(n)$ small with $n$ no larger than a fixed multiple of $\rho(\beta)^{-1}$? The preliminary estimate alone will not do this. It will instead supply a reference box for the comparison between cutoffs.

<a id="pre:mixing"></a>
### Preliminary mixing

<span id="pre:section" label="pre:section"></span>

We first prove a lattice estimate whose length scale is less precise than the natural continuum scale, but whose uniformity under weakening bonds will be needed later. Spins take values in the unit sphere $S^3\subset\mathbb R^4$, identified with the unit quaternions. The a priori measure is normalized Haar measure. For a finite graph in the square lattice the Gibbs density is proportional to $$\exp\left(\sum_{e=xy}\beta_e q_x\cdot q_y\right).$$ Each unoriented edge occurs once. A free subgraph means that bonds outside the graph are absent; it imposes no pinned spin values.

For a local Lipschitz observable $F$, supported on a finite set $A$, put $$L_F=\left\lVert F\right\rVert_\infty+\max_{x\in A}\operatorname{Lip}_x(F),$$ where the individual Lipschitz constant uses round distance on $S^3$ with all other spins fixed. Distances between supports use the maximum norm, or its periodic version on a torus.

<a id="stmt-thm-preliminary"></a>
<div id="thm:preliminary" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (Preliminary mixing). *There are fixed positive constants $C,c,p$ such that, on setting <a id="eq:pre:Xi"></a>$$\Xi(\beta)=\exp\left[\pi\beta+
 C\sqrt{(1+\beta)\log(2+\beta)}\right],
 \label{eq:pre:Xi}$$ the following hold for every $\beta\geq0$.*

*For every unpinned rectangular torus with each side at least $C\Xi(\beta)$, and every choice $0\leq\beta_e\leq\beta$, local observables $F,G$ supported on $A,A'$ at distance $d$ satisfy <a id="eq:pre:theorem-mixing"></a>$$|\operatorname{Cov}(F,G)|\leq C(1+\beta+|A|+|A'|+d)^{20}
 L_FL_G e^{-cd/\Xi(\beta)}.
 \label{eq:pre:theorem-mixing}$$ The same bound holds on every unpinned free subgraph, with ambient lattice distance. In the homogeneous model the local limit of periodic square measures is unique.*

<a id="eq:pre:theorem-delta"></a>
*Let $Z_\beta(n,m)$ be the partition function of the homogeneous $n$-by-$m$ torus. For every even $n\geq C\Xi(\beta)$, $$0\leq\Delta_\beta(n):=4\log Z_\beta(n,n)-\log Z_\beta(2n,2n)
 \leq C(1+\beta)^pn^p e^{-cn/\Xi(\beta)}.
 \label{eq:pre:theorem-delta}$$*

</div>

The proof first obtains a small stiffness on a suitable pinned square. This part allows arbitrary signed couplings and fixed right twists. We then use coarse orientation variables to prove decay for charged observables. Two conditional decompositions into ferromagnetic circle and Ising systems give mixing for arbitrary local observables. Only this last part requires nonnegative untwisted couplings.

Constants denoted by $C$ may increase from line to line. All are independent of lattice size and of the particular couplings and pins. Dependence on a fixed moment order or a fixed auxiliary exponent is indicated when needed. For continuum test fields on the unit square, $\|\cdot\|_2$ denotes the Lebesgue $L^2$ norm. For random variables, $\|\cdot\|_p$ denotes the probabilistic $L^p$ norm.

<a id="pre:local"></a>
#### Local class and Ward identities

The following calculation is a local Ward identity, in the tradition of (Mermin 1967; Driessler, Landau, and Perez 1979; Aizenman and Simon 1980) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Mermin1967](#ref-Mermin1967), [DriesslerLandauPerez](#ref-DriesslerLandauPerez), [AizenmanSimonWard](#ref-AizenmanSimonWard)]. We give the full quaternion formula because the noncommuting current term and its factor two determine the quantitative stiffness gain.

Work on $Q(S)=\{0,\ldots,S\}^2$ with all boundary spins fixed. For each positively oriented edge $e=x\to y$, set

$$t_e+s_e=q_yU_eq_x^{-1},\qquad t_e\in\mathbb R,\quad s_e\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb H\simeq\mathbb R^3,$$

where $U_e$ is any fixed unit quaternion. Take signed coefficients $|b_e|\leq b\leq B$, with $w_e=b_e$ except that edges tangent to the boundary carry $w_e=b_e/2$. The local density is $Z^{-1}\exp(\sum_e w_et_e)$. The half allocation makes adjacent cell actions sum exactly to the action on the union.

For macroscopic midpoint coordinates $z_e$, define

$$X_Q(F)=S^{-1}\sum_e w_es_e\cdot F_{\mu(e)}(z_e),$$

<a id="eq:pre:1-1"></a>
$$\mathcal H_Q(F,G)=S^{-2}\mathbb E\sum_e w_et_e\,\overline{F_e}\cdot G_e
 -\operatorname{Cov}(X_Q(F),X_Q(G)).
\label{eq:pre:1-1}$$

The covariance is sesquilinear, with conjugation on the first entry. Write $H_Q$ for the restriction to constant $3\times2$ matrices. In each spatial direction the sum of the absolute edge coefficients is at most $bS^2$: there are $S(S-1)$ interior edges and two tangent boundary rows with half weight. Thus

<a id="eq:pre:1-2"></a>$$H_Q\leq bI.
\label{eq:pre:1-2}$$

This is valid with signed coefficients and twists. No positive lower bound on $H_Q$ is claimed or used.

For an imaginary unit $a$, rotate $q_x\mapsto e^{af_x}q_x$. A bond quaternion changes to $e^{af_y}(t_e+s_e)e^{-af_x}$. Its $(1,a)$-plane rotates through $f_y-f_x$; its imaginary plane perpendicular to $a$ rotates through $f_y+f_x$. If $\mathcal A=-\sum w_et_e$, then

<a id="eq:pre:1-3"></a>
$$D_f\mathcal A=\sum_e w_e(s_e\cdot a)(f_y-f_x),\qquad
 D_f^2\mathcal A=\sum_e w_et_e(f_y-f_x)^2.
\label{eq:pre:1-3}$$

For a general infinitesimal imaginary profile $\eta_x$, the imaginary bond derivative is exactly

<a id="eq:pre:1-4"></a>$$D_\eta s_e=t_e(\eta_y-\eta_x)+(\eta_y+\eta_x)\times s_e.
\label{eq:pre:1-4}$$

Consequently the compact-profile Ward identity has a contact term and a bracket term. If $\eta$ vanishes on the fixed boundary, Haar integration by parts gives

<a id="eq:pre:1-5"></a>
$$\mathbb E[\overline{X_Q(d_S\eta)}X_Q(F)]
=S^{-2}\mathbb E\sum_e w_et_e\overline{(d_S\eta)_e}\cdot F_e
+\mathbb E X_Q\!\left(F_e\times(\overline{\eta_y}+\overline{\eta_x})\right),
\label{eq:pre:1-5}$$

where the cross product is extended complex bilinearly. This follows first for real profiles and then by complex linear extension. Here $(d_S\eta)_\mu(z)=S[\eta(z+e_\mu/(2S))-\eta(z-e_\mu/(2S))]$. This fixes the normalization and the factor two in the eventual nonabelian derivative. For the same-axis score there is no bracket term; <a href="#eq:pre:1-3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:1-3">[eq:pre:1-3]</a> follows directly.

<a id="pre:moments"></a>
#### Current moments near a pinned boundary

Assume throughout the local induction

<a id="eq:pre:2-1"></a>
$$\log(2+S)\leq16B.
\label{eq:pre:2-1}$$

<a id="stmt-pre-current-moments"></a>
<div id="pre:current-moments" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Uniform current moments). *For every fixed $1\leq p<\infty$,*

<a id="eq:pre:2-2"></a>
*$$\|X_Q(F)\|_p\leq C_p(1+b)\log(2+S)\|F\|_{C^1}.
\label{eq:pre:2-2}$$*

*Here and subsequently the derivative norm is in the indicated rescaled coordinates. The constants are independent of pins, signs, twists, and side length.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-current-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-current-moments</summary>

*Proof.*

<a id="pre:part-2-1"></a>
##### Compact scores

For a real one-axis profile $f$ zero at the pinned boundary, the second variation along the full rotation path is bounded in absolute value by $b\sum_e(f_y-f_x)^2$. Taylor’s formula and invariance of the Haar integral under the compact rotation imply

<a id="eq:pre:2-3"></a>
$$\mathbb E\exp(tD_f\mathcal A)
\leq\exp\left[\tfrac12 b t^2\sum_e(f_y-f_x)^2\right].
\label{eq:pre:2-3}$$

Here $E(f)=\sum_e(f_y-f_x)^2$ and $q^{tf}_x=e^{atf_x}q_x$. Use $\mathcal A(q^{-tf})\leq\mathcal A(q)-tD_f\mathcal A(q)+\frac12bt^2E(f)$, exponentiate, and integrate. The same Taylor estimate bounds the $r$-th moment of the likelihood ratio of a unit rotation by $\exp(C_r bE(f))$. It is therefore bounded when $bE(f)$ is bounded. These arguments use $|w_et_e|\leq b$, so remain valid for signed and twisted interactions.

<a id="pre:part-2-2"></a>
##### Extracting a transverse current on a buffered patch

Take an interior patch of lattice side $d$, with a proportional buffer. For a scalar test $h$ on the inner patch, let $H$ be its $C^1$ norm in patch coordinates. Choose a compact scalar potential $\vartheta$, affine with slope $1/d$ in direction $\mu$ on all edges meeting the support of $h$, and with bounded Dirichlet energy. Choose $a$ perpendicular to the current color to be extracted. The compact score in the other perpendicular color has every fixed moment $O_p(\sqrt b)$ by <a href="#eq:pre:2-3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:2-3">[eq:pre:2-3]</a>.

Assume $H>0$, since otherwise the test is zero. Rotate this score by the two opposite profiles

$$f=\tfrac12\arcsin(h/N),\qquad N=C\sqrt{1+b}\,H.$$

The rotations have uniformly bounded likelihood norms. The difference of the rotated scores is the transverse current multiplied by $2\sin(f_x+f_y)$. Multiplication by $N$ extracts $h$ at the midpoint up to $CH/d$. It is important that only this first-order error is needed: after the score normalization $d^{-1}$, its deterministic total over $O(d^2)$ edges is $O(bH)$. The moment cost of the two scores multiplied by $N$ is $O_p((1+b)H)$. Hence

<a id="eq:pre:2-4"></a>
$$\left\|d^{-1}\sum_{e\parallel\mu}w_e(s_e)_k h_e\right\|_p
\leq C_p(1+b)H.
\label{eq:pre:2-4}$$

Bounded $d$ can be treated deterministically.

<a id="pre:part-2-3"></a>
##### Whitney summation

A width $O(1)$ boundary layer contributes $O(b\|F\|_\infty)$. Decompose the remaining square into buffered patches with $d$ comparable to distance to the boundary. At one dyadic scale there are $O(S/d)$ such patches; their normalized tests have $C^1$ norms $O(\|F\|_{C^1})$. Applying <a href="#eq:pre:2-4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:2-4">[eq:pre:2-4]</a>, multiplying by $d/S$, and summing gives $O((1+b)\|F\|_{C^1})$ per scale. There are $O(\log(2+S))$ scales. This proves <a href="#eq:pre:2-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:2-2">[eq:pre:2-2]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pre:cells"></a>
#### Conditioning into cells

The symbol $L$ in this preliminary argument is an auxiliary integer which may change during the stiffness iteration. It is independent of the fixed block size chosen for the exact renormalization map in Section <a href="#free:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="free:section">4</a>.

The current-moment estimate permits replacing slowly varying fields by their values at cell anchors. We next express the large-square stiffness through the conditioned cell laws; the variance of their conditional currents is the gain to exploit.

Tile $Q(S)$ by $L^2$ cells of side $R=S/L$. Condition on their complete boundary rings $\omega$. Cell interiors are independent and the ring density has action $S_g(\omega)=-\sum_c\log Z_c(\omega)$. Set

$$j(F)=\mathbb E[X_Q(F)\mid\omega]
=L^{-1}\sum_c\mathbb E_cX_c(F).$$

Let $z_c$ be the lower-left cell anchor and put

$$N_c(F)=\sup_{c^+}(|F|+L^{-1}|\nabla F|),\qquad
D_c(F)=L^{-1}N_c(\nabla F).$$

The fixed enlargement $c^+$ is used only for smooth test-field estimates. Write $P(B)$ for a fixed polynomial whose degree and coefficients may depend on fixed moment orders or prescribed exponents, but not on $R,L,S$. Equation <a href="#eq:pre:2-2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:2-2">[eq:pre:2-2]</a> and <a href="#eq:pre:2-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:2-1">[eq:pre:2-1]</a> give

<a id="eq:pre:3-1"></a>
$$|\mathbb E_cX_c(F)|\leq P(B)N_c(F),\quad
|\mathcal H_c(F,G)|\leq P(B)N_c(F)N_c(G),
\label{eq:pre:3-1}$$

<a id="eq:pre:3-2"></a>
$$|\mathcal H_c(F,G)-\overline{F_c}H_cG_c|
\leq P(B)[D_c(F)N_c(G)+N_c(F)D_c(G)].
\label{eq:pre:3-2}$$

For the last estimate subtract the constants $F_c,G_c$ in the bilinear form. Their remainders have cell-coordinate $C^1$ norm bounded by the displayed $D_c$. One can instead use a direct Lipschitz bound on the cell where appropriate.

For a scalar ring rotation $u$, let $H_c^u$ be the resulting constant-field stiffness and $O_c(u)=\operatorname{Ad}(e^{au(z_c)})$. Then

<a id="eq:pre:3-3"></a>
$$\|O_c(u)^TH_c^uO_c(u)-H_c\|\leq P(B)D_c(u),
\label{eq:pre:3-3}$$

and its derivative at zero satisfies the same bound. To prove this, remove the constant rotation $u(z_c)$ exactly by covariance. Along the remaining path also rotate the interior integration variables. The logarithmic density derivative is the negative of the centered cell current with test $aR(u_y-u_x)$, whose fixed moments are bounded by $P(B)D_c(u)$. The residual profile need not vanish on the cell boundary; the current-moment bound applies to this test without that restriction. Derivatives of a constant-field current have the bracket current with residual profile of size $D_c(u)$, plus a deterministic contact of that size. Differentiate the contact and covariance in <a href="#eq:pre:1-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:1-1">[eq:pre:1-1]</a> and apply Hölder with the already established current moments. Restarting this calculation at each point of the path gives the bound for arbitrary amplitude $u$; there is no exponential dependence on its constant part.

Rotating all rings while fixing the outside boundary gives the exact Hessian identity

<a id="eq:pre:3-4"></a>
$$D_u^2S_g=L^{-2}\sum_c\mathcal H_c(a\,d_Su,a\,d_Su).
\label{eq:pre:3-4}$$

<a id="pre:gaussian"></a>
#### Gaussian augmentation and commuting derivatives

The cell stiffness matrices need not be positive. We add auxiliary Gaussian noise so that the desired upper bound on $H_Q$ becomes a lower bound on the variance of an augmented current. The identity below makes this reduction exact.

Suppose uniformly throughout the pinned cell class $H_c\leq hI$, where $B^{-D}\leq h\leq B$. Choose

<a id="eq:pre:4-1"></a>
$$h<v\leq2B,\qquad c_0\geq B^{-D'},\qquad
C_c=vI-H_c-c_0P_a\geq B^{-D'}I,
\label{eq:pre:4-1}$$

where $P_a$ is projection onto the two spatial components of color $a$. Let $n_c$ be independent standard six-dimensional Gaussian vectors, and $n_{0,c}$ independent two-dimensional Gaussian vectors of covariance $c_0I$, all independent of the true ring data. Define

<a id="eq:pre:4-2"></a>$$J(F)=j(F)+L^{-1}\sum_c(\sqrt{C_c}n_c+a n_{0,c})\cdot F_c.
\label{eq:pre:4-2}$$

For every constant real matrix $A$, total variance gives exactly

<a id="eq:pre:4-3"></a>
$$H_Q(A,A)=v|A|^2-\operatorname{Var}J(A).
\label{eq:pre:4-3}$$

Indeed $H_Q=L^{-2}\sum_c\mathbb EH_c-\operatorname{Var}j$, and the conditional noise covariance adds $vI-L^{-2}\sum_cH_c$.

Choose a real trigonometric cutoff $\psi$, zero on the boundary, with band $O(m)$. Let $f_i=\psi\varphi_i$, where $\varphi_i$ are the real orthonormal Fourier modes of frequency $|k|\leq4K$, including the constant. Use $K+m\ll L$ and

$$G_0=L^{-2}\sum_c\nabla f(z_c)\nabla f(z_c)^T,
\quad
p=(c_0G_0)^{-1}L^{-1}\sum_c\nabla f(z_c)n_{0,c},
\quad \theta=f\cdot p.$$

Quadrature is exact at this band, so $G_0$ is the continuum gradient Gram matrix. It is positive: a linear combination of the $f_i$ with zero gradient is a constant, zero on the boundary, and hence is zero; division by $\psi$ on its open nonzero set then annihilates the Fourier polynomial. Thus $p$ is Gaussian with covariance $(c_0G_0)^{-1}$.

Put

$$\omega^0=e^{-a\theta}\omega,\qquad
n_{0,c}^0=n_{0,c}-c_0\nabla\theta(z_c)/L,$$

$$n_c^0=O_c(\theta)^Tn_c-\sqrt{C_c^0}\,a\nabla\theta(z_c)/L,$$

where $C_c^0$ uses $\omega^0$. At fixed base data $(\omega^0,n_0^0,n^0)$, the density of $p$ is proportional to $e^{-V(p)}$, with

<a id="eq:pre:4-4"></a>
$$V(p)=\tfrac12c_0p^TG_0p+S_g(e^{a\theta}\omega^0)
+\tfrac12\sum_c|n_c^0+\sqrt{C_c^0}\,a\nabla\theta(z_c)/L|^2.
\label{eq:pre:4-4}$$

This is an exact change of variables. First split the Gaussian $n_0$ into its linear projection and orthogonal residual. At fixed $p$, the ring rotation preserves product Haar measure. At fixed $p,\omega^0$, the $n_c$ transformations are orthogonal maps followed by translations. These successive maps have constant Jacobian; dependencies of later maps on earlier variables give a triangular Jacobian, not an extra determinant.

For fixed scalar directions $u_i$ in the potential span, let $\partial_i$ mean addition of $tu_i$ to $\theta$ with all base data fixed. They commute. Conditional integration by parts gives, for $\partial_i^*=-\partial_i+\partial_iV$,

<a id="eq:pre:4-5"></a>
$$\mathbb E\left(\sum_i\partial_i^*Y_i\right)^2
=\mathbb E\sum_{ij}(\partial_jY_i)(\partial_iY_j)
+\mathbb E\sum_{ij}Y_i(\partial_i\partial_jV)Y_j.
\label{eq:pre:4-5}$$

The identity first holds conditionally and then in the full law. The conditional density has Gaussian decay: its first term is strictly positive quadratic and $S_g$ is bounded for each fixed finite system. The functions involved and their derivatives have at most polynomial growth in Gaussian variables, since the matrices remain in a fixed positive cone for that system. Thus the integration by parts has no missing boundary term.

<a id="pre:bands"></a>
#### Three uniform band estimates

The integration-by-parts identity in the preceding subsection contains a Hessian term and a crossed derivative term. We next control their ingredients uniformly in the number of modes, while retaining the exact contact cancellation in the Ward identity.

Fix $D,D'$ and any requested power $N$. Take $K/L\leq B^{-J}$, with $J$ sufficiently large depending only on these fixed numbers and on the fixed test amplitude bounds. Retain $S\geq L$, <a href="#eq:pre:2-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:2-1">[eq:pre:2-1]</a>, and $Bm\leq K$. The constants denoted $P(B)$ below do not depend on the subsequently increased $J$.

<a id="pre:part-5-1"></a>
##### Sampling, gradients, and the good event

For any trigonometric field of band $O(K)$, with $K\ll L$, we have

<a id="eq:pre:5-1"></a>
$$\|N(F)\|_\square\leq C\|F\|_2,\qquad
\|D(F)\|_\square\leq C(K/L)\|F\|_2,
\quad
\|a\|_\square^2=L^{-2}\sum_c|a_c|^2.
\label{eq:pre:5-1}$$

Here is a direct sampling proof. Choose a smooth frequency cutoff equal to one on the allowed band and supported in a ball of radius $cL$, with fixed $c$. Its periodic convolution kernel $K_L$ satisfies, for any fixed $N_0>2$, $$|\partial^r K_L(z)|\leq C_{r,N_0}L^{2+r}
 (1+L\operatorname{dist}(z,\mathbb Z^2))^{-N_0}.$$ This follows by writing the kernel as the periodization of the rescaled inverse Fourier transform of the smooth cutoff and integrating that transform by parts. Since $F=K_L*F$, weighted Cauchy gives $$\sup_{z\in c^+}|F(z)|^2\leq
 CL^2\int (1+L\operatorname{dist}(z_c-y,\mathbb Z^2))^{-N_0}|F(y)|^2\,dy.$$ Sum over cells and divide by $L^2$; the remaining lattice sum is bounded. Apply the same argument to derivatives and use Parseval, $\|\partial^rF\|_2\leq(CK)^r\|F\|_2$. This proves <a href="#eq:pre:5-1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:5-1">[eq:pre:5-1]</a>, including its constant independent of the number of cells. The centered-difference Fourier multiplier also gives

<a id="eq:pre:5-2"></a>
$$\|d_Su-\nabla u\|_2\leq C(K/S)^2\|\nabla u\|_2.
\label{eq:pre:5-2}$$

Although the matrix $G_0$ need not be well conditioned in the chosen basis, that is immaterial. The covariance of $\nabla\theta$ is $c_0^{-1}$ times the orthogonal projection onto the gradient subspace. Evaluation of a band $O(K)$ gradient field has norm at most $CK$, and evaluation after one more derivative at most $CK^2$. Therefore

<a id="eq:pre:5-3"></a>
$$\rho=L^{-1}\|\nabla\theta\|_\infty+L^{-2}\|\nabla^2\theta\|_\infty
\leq B^{-n}
\label{eq:pre:5-3}$$
